### 2026-09-13 — SDK response decoding: TunnelClient fixed as scoped, client.ts confirmed not applicable but fixed anyway for a different real bug, public type widened

**Decided by:** Claude Code (bodyEncoding follow-up session)
**Context:** context.md risk #21 / decision.md's 2026-09-12 "bodyEncoding" entry left the SDK-side decode of `sdk:response`'s `bodyEncoding` field explicitly unfixed. The brief asked to investigate first — check whether `TunnelClient` or `client.ts` had any existing encoding handling, and confirm whether `bodyEncoding` was even relevant to `client.ts`'s path before assuming a fix was needed there.
**Investigation:** `TunnelClient.onMessage()` passed `msg.body` straight through with no decoding at all — confirmed the bug as described. `client.ts` (`VhyxvoidClient`) talks HTTP directly to the tunnel subdomain via `fetch()` — it never receives an `sdk:response` message, so `bodyEncoding` genuinely doesn't apply to it; the bytes it receives over the wire are already correct once `HttpTunnelHandler.writeResponse()`'s response-path fix runs (real bytes, real Content-Type, no base64/field wrapper at the HTTP layer). This confirms the brief's suspicion that this path might need nothing — **but** `client.ts`'s own response-parsing code (`res.text()` for any non-`application/json` content-type) is itself lossy for binary content, a separate, real, previously-unflagged bug found by actually reading the code rather than assuming "not relevant to bodyEncoding" meant "nothing to fix here." Also found the identical `toString('utf8')` pattern in `LocalAgentClient`, which `TunnelClient.request()` calls first (local-discovery fast path, bypasses the hub) — same bug class, no `bodyEncoding` field involved since it never touches the hub's protocol at all.
**Decision — fix all three, not just the one literally named:** `TunnelClient.onMessage()` decodes `bodyEncoding: 'base64'` into a real `Buffer`; `LocalAgentClient.httpRequest()` and `client.ts`'s `request()` both now detect binary via content-type and return real bytes instead of a UTF-8-mangled string. Rationale: the actual goal here is "binary responses work correctly across the SDK's real response-reading code paths," not "the literal `bodyEncoding` field is consumed somewhere" — fixing only `TunnelClient` while leaving `client.ts` (the *primary, default* SDK surface per the SDK Client Strategy section) and `LocalAgentClient` (reachable from `TunnelClient.request()` itself) still corrupting binary responses would have been a materially incomplete close-out of this item.
**Public API tradeoff — flagged as instructed:** `TunnelResponse.body` (packages/sdk's public type, used by both `TunnelClient` and `LocalAgentClient`) widened from `string | null` to `string | Buffer | null`. Checked before deciding: `client.ts`'s `ClientResponse<T>.data: T` was already generic (no change needed there). Grepped this entire monorepo for consumers of `TunnelResponse` — only `packages/sdk` itself defines/exports it; nothing in `apps/web`, `packages/middleware`, or `packages/next` imports or narrows it. Combined with the fact that binary responses via any of these three paths were already broken (either mangled utf8 or an undecoded base64 string) before this fix, no existing *working* caller pattern can regress from this widening — a caller that assumed `body` was always a string was already receiving wrong data for binary responses, not a valid one. This is a deliberate, scoped type change, not an incidental one — recorded here per the brief's explicit instruction to flag rather than force it through unannounced.
**Consolidation:** the binary-content-type detection list, independently duplicated across `LocalAgentClient` and `client.ts` while writing this fix (on top of the pre-existing copy in `BackendProxy`), was pulled into `packages/protocol/src/bodyEncoding.ts` (`isBinaryContentType`) — see the companion request-direction entry below for the full rationale; both fixes touch this consolidation together.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/sdkResponseBodyEncoding.test.ts` — `TunnelClient` against a real `ws.WebSocketServer` standing in for the hub (binary PNG decodes to a byte-identical `Buffer`, JSON stays a string); `LocalAgentClient` and `client.ts`/`VhyxvoidClient` against a real local HTTP server (same two cases each).
**Status:** active — context.md risk #21's first follow-up (a) closed

---

### 2026-09-13 — Request-direction body corruption: symmetric fix, WS SDK path confirmed out of scope (can't carry binary today)

**Decided by:** Claude Code (bodyEncoding follow-up session)
**Context:** context.md risk #21 / decision.md's 2026-09-12 "bodyEncoding" entry also left `HttpTunnelHandler.readBody()`'s mirrored request-direction bug unfixed, explicitly because "fixing this symmetrically requires auditing the SDK's own request-body encoding assumptions too (does it already base64 anything before sending? unverified)." The brief asked to investigate that exact question before deciding whether the fix needed to be symmetric (hub decodes what the SDK already encodes) or one-sided (SDK itself also needs a sending-side fix).
**Investigation:** `TunnelClient.request()` does `bodyStr = params.body != null ? JSON.stringify(params.body) : null` unconditionally — the WS SDK path can only ever send JSON-serializable values, never raw binary, and never has, so there is no existing SDK-side base64 encoding to mirror and no way for a caller to trigger this bug through `TunnelClient` today. The actual corrupted path is the raw HTTP tunnel path — any HTTP client (curl, a webhook provider, `client.ts`, or literally a browser `<form>`) POSTing a binary body directly to `accountslug--label.vhyxvoid.com`, which `HttpTunnelHandler.readBody()` receives and unconditionally `.toString('utf8')`'d.
**Decision:** Fix is one-sided, not symmetric — the hub's own request-reading code is the actual bug, not something the SDK needs to change to cooperate with. Enabling binary request bodies over the WS SDK path itself (changing `TunnelClient.request()`'s signature to accept a `Buffer`) would be a real, separate feature addition, not a bug fix — noted but explicitly not done, since nothing asked for that capability and it would be unannounced public API surface growth beyond what this task scoped.
**Implementation:** `TunnelForwardMsg` gained the mirror-image optional `bodyEncoding?: 'utf8'|'base64'` field. `HttpTunnelHandler.readBody()` now returns the raw `Buffer` instead of decoding inline; `handle()` decides `utf8` vs `base64` from the request's own `content-type` header (same detection used for the response direction) and sets `bodyEncoding` on the constructed `TunnelForwardMsg`. `BackendProxy.forward()` decodes a `'base64'` `msg.body` back into a real `Buffer` before passing it to axios as `data` (previously always passed the raw string, which — once the hub started sending base64 text for binary bodies — would have sent the base64 *text* to the local backend instead of the original file; before this session nothing sent bodyEncoding on requests at all, so this was latent, not yet triggered in production). An older hub that never sets `bodyEncoding` is treated as `utf8`, matching the pre-fix behavior exactly — verified with a dedicated test.
**Not touched:** `Message.router.ts`'s `handleSdkRequest()` (WS path) builds its own `TunnelForwardMsg` from `SdkRequestMsg.body` and does not set `bodyEncoding` — correct as-is, since that body is always the JSON-stringified text confirmed above; `BackendProxy`'s fallback (`bodyEncoding` undefined → treat as `utf8`) already handles this correctly with zero changes needed on that path.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/tunnelRequestBodyEncoding.test.ts` — `HttpTunnelHandler.handle()`'s encode side (against fake registries + a real HTTP request, binary/JSON/bodyless-GET cases), `BackendProxy.forward()`'s decode side (binary/utf8/missing-bodyEncoding-treated-as-utf8 cases) against a real local backend server, and a full in-process round trip (hub encode → agent decode → real backend receives the exact original PNG bytes) — the literal scenario the brief asked to verify.
**Status:** active — context.md risk #21's second follow-up (b) closed; item is now fully resolved, both originally-flagged gaps closed

### 2026-09-13 — packages/sdk barrel export gap: committed the existing (uncommitted) fix, left the primary/secondary path split for its own session

**Decided by:** Claude Code (export-gap fix session)
**Context:** The previous (bodyEncoding) session's report flagged `packages/sdk/src/index.ts` as having "an unrelated diff mixed in" that it correctly excluded from its own commit. This session was asked to investigate that diff before touching anything, rather than assume it was noise — specifically to determine whether it was a separate, already-decided piece of work worth committing on its own, or an abandoned experiment.
**Investigation:** `git diff` against HEAD showed the entire uncommitted diff was: two organizational comments ("Existing WebSocket SDK" / "HTTP client SDK"), some blank-line tightening, and — the substance — `export { createClient, VhyxvoidClient, ClientError } from "./client"` plus `export type { ClientConfig, ClientResponse } from "./client"`. There was no second, different piece of work tangled in; the "unrelated" framing from the last session meant "unrelated to bodyEncoding," not "unrelated to anything." Cross-checked context.md's risk #12 and session_update.md's 2026-09-12 reconciliation entry: both already describe this exact state — "`packages/sdk/src/index.ts` now exports both `TunnelClient`... and `client.ts`'s `createClient`/`VhyxvoidClient`... from the same barrel" — meaning this diff has existed, uncommitted, since at least that session (2026-09-12), and is real, deliberate, previously-analyzed work, not an abandoned experiment. It directly implements the "client.ts's exports should be reachable" half of decision.md's 2026-09-09 SDK Client Strategy decision — just not the "narrowed to a secondary `@vhyxvoid/sdk/ws` path" half.
**Verification before committing:** confirmed all of `client.ts`'s public exports are covered (nothing missing), confirmed `TunnelClient`/`TunnelError`/`TunnelTimeoutError`'s existing exports are untouched, ran a real build (`tsc -b`) and confirmed `dist/index.js` actually contains all six exports, and added `tests/e2e/sdkBarrelExports.test.ts` — a real construct-and-check smoke test (not just an import check) for both `createClient`'s `VhyxvoidClient` and `TunnelClient`, run against `HEAD`'s version first to confirm it genuinely fails there (0 references to `createClient`) before confirming it passes against the fix.
**Decision — commit the fix, don't also do the primary/secondary repackaging:** the task at hand was "the barrel export gap," which this diff fully closes — a consumer importing from `@vhyxvoid/sdk` can reach both `createClient` and `TunnelClient` today. The fuller `@vhyxvoid/sdk` (default) / `@vhyxvoid/sdk/ws` (secondary) packaging split from the 2026-09-09 decision is a distinct, larger piece of work (a new subpath export in `package.json`, doc updates, deciding what happens to internal consumers that already import `TunnelClient` from the main path) that a previous session explicitly recommended "finishing in one pass rather than leaving it half-migrated" — taking that at face value, folding a partial attempt at it into this narrow export-gap fix would risk exactly the half-migrated state that recommendation warned against. Left as its own, still-open item.
**Also found, noted but not fixed (out of scope for this fix):** `packages/sdk/package.json`'s `exports` field declares `"import": "./dist/index.mjs"`, but the package's build script (`tsc -b`, CommonJS-only per `tsconfig.base.json`) never emits an `.mjs` file — confirmed via `ls dist/` after a real build. This predates this session's change (the `import` condition was never satisfiable for any of the package's exports, not just the newly-added ones) — a separate, pre-existing ESM-build tooling gap, flagged for whoever next touches this package's build setup.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/sdkBarrelExports.test.ts` — `createClient`/`VhyxvoidClient`/`ClientError` reachable and constructible; `TunnelClient`/`TunnelError`/`TunnelTimeoutError` still reachable and constructible.
**Status:** active — context.md risk #12 narrowed: barrel-export gap FIXED, primary/secondary path split remains OPEN as its own task

### 2026-09-13 — Billing model: flat-rate tiers, usage tracking stays display-only, no metered billing

**Decided by:** Tanveer
**Context:** context.md risks #6 and #36 had been blocked on a billing-model decision since the 2026-09-12 reconciliation pass — `HardcodedPlanLimitService` always returned PRO for every account because nothing had decided whether the system should be flat-rate (a fixed set of plan tiers, each with fixed limits) or metered (usage-based billing reported to Stripe per period).
**Decision:** Flat-rate tiers only (FREE/PRO/ENTERPRISE, already real — Stripe price IDs and `PLAN_LIMITS` per-tier numeric values both already existed and are unchanged by this decision). Usage tracking (Redis → `UsageAggregate` via `FlushUsageWorker`, already real and running) stays purely for dashboard display — it does not drive billing and there is no plan to make it do so. No metered billing.
**Implementation — plan-limit lookup fixed:** `HardcodedPlanLimitService.getLimitsForAccount()` (renamed `SubscriptionPlanLimitService`, since the old name described a bug that no longer exists) now looks up the account's most-recent `Subscription` row (reusing the exact "most recent wins" convention `PrismaSubscriptionRepository.findByAccountId` already established for the billing dashboard, since `Subscription.accountId` is not unique — an account can accumulate more than one row over time) and returns `PLAN_LIMITS[subscription.plan]`. Confirmed no drift between the Prisma schema's `Plan` enum and `PLAN_LIMITS`'s keys before wiring them together — both are exactly `FREE`/`PRO`/`ENTERPRISE`; the codebase already has an established `row.plan as Plan` cast pattern (`PrismaSubscriptionRepository.toEntity()`) for crossing between Prisma's generated `Plan` enum and the domain module's own hand-written `Plan` enum, reused here rather than inventing a new conversion approach.
**Edge cases, decided explicitly (per the brief's instruction not to assume):**
- **No `Subscription` row at all → `FREE`.** Verified against how account creation actually works, not assumed: `CreateOrganization.usecase.ts` never creates a `Subscription` row; the only code path that ever creates one is `HandleStripeWebhookUseCase`'s `customer.subscription.created` handler. So "no subscription" unambiguously means "never been through Stripe checkout," i.e. still on the default free tier.
- **`Account.status` of `SUSPENDED`/`RESTRICTED`/`CANCELED`/`DELETED` → downgraded to `FREE`, regardless of what `Subscription.plan` says.** Checked first whether this was already gated elsewhere, per the brief's explicit instruction not to duplicate existing logic: grepped `CreateApiKey`/`RotateApiKey`/`UpdateApiKey` (the three call sites of `getLimitsForAccount`) and their shared `resolveMembership` helper — none of them check `Account.status` at all, only membership/role level. So this is not a duplicate; it's the one real enforcement point for this specific call path. `Account.status` was chosen as the gating signal (rather than re-deriving from `Subscription.status` directly) because it's already the canonical, webhook-maintained signal for "is this account currently in good standing" (`HandleStripeWebhookUseCase.subscriptionStatusToAccountStatus()` syncs it on every Stripe subscription event) — recomputing the same judgment from `Subscription.status` independently would be the exact kind of duplicated logic the brief warned against.
- **`PAST_DUE` deliberately excluded from the downgrade list — keeps the real plan's limits.** This is the grace-period window: `GRACE_PERIOD_DAYS`/`GRACE_PERIOD_MS`/`Account.graceEndsAt` and the webhook's `handleInvoicePaymentFailed` (sets `graceEndsAt = now + GRACE_PERIOD_MS` on the first failed payment) already establish that a `PAST_DUE` account is meant to keep working during a short grace window, not be immediately punished for a single missed payment. Only once the grace period actually expires does the intended design move the account to `SUSPENDED` (`GracePeriodWorker`), which the downgrade list does catch.
- **Missing `Account` row entirely → `FREE`** (data-integrity fallback, not expected in practice since `accountId` always comes from an authenticated actor's own membership — fails to the most restrictive tier rather than throwing and blocking the request).
**New gap surfaced by this investigation, not fixed this session:** `GracePeriodWorker` — the class that's supposed to actually move a `PAST_DUE` account to `SUSPENDED` once `graceEndsAt` passes — is fully written and correct, but is never instantiated or scheduled anywhere in the codebase (confirmed by grep: zero references outside its own file). This means the `PAST_DUE`-keeps-plan-limits behavior decided above is correct as designed, but in practice today a `PAST_DUE` account has no automatic path to ever leaving that state — it would keep its real plan's limits indefinitely rather than for the intended 7-day window. Recorded as context.md item 41, not fixed here: scheduling it correctly (boot-sequence placement, whether to sweep already-stale `PAST_DUE` accounts on first deploy) is a real, separate, contained task that deserves its own verification pass.
**Dead-code decision — speculative metered-billing infrastructure removed:** No class literally named `ReportUsageToStripeWorker` exists (searched the whole repo) — but its equivalent orphaned plumbing did: `UsageAggregate.markReportedToStripe()` and `UsageAggregateRepository.findUnreportedToStripe()` (both zero callers anywhere, confirmed by grep), plus two fully-commented-out Prisma schema models (`UsageAggregate` — an earlier draft — and `UsageReport`, a dedicated Stripe-reporting-status table) describing the same abandoned design. None of this was "running and silently failing" — it was never invoked at all, so there was no active bug to stop, just dead code describing a design now formally decided against. Removed the two zero-caller methods outright. Kept the `reportedToStripe`/`stripeUsageRecordId` Prisma columns and their index (dropping DB columns needs a real migration, which this decision doesn't itself warrant — a comment now marks them vestigial and safe to drop whenever this table is next migrated for an unrelated reason) and deleted the two dead commented-out schema models as drive-by cleanup. `lockPeriod`/`lockedAt`/`isLocked` on the same entity are also currently zero-caller and tied to the same "immutable before Stripe invoicing" rationale, but were deliberately left untouched — `lockedAt` is described generically as "billing period finalized," not exclusively Stripe-specific, and removing it would touch `incrementQuantity`'s guard too; flagged here as a related observation rather than acted on, since the brief's ask was specifically about the Stripe-reporting worker, not this adjacent mechanism.
**Confirmed, not touched: usage dashboard is genuinely display-only.** `GetApiKeyUsageUseCase` (backend) is a pure read query, and `toUsageDTO()` doesn't expose `reportedToStripe`/`isLocked`/`stripeUsageRecordId` to the frontend at all. `apps/web`'s `useTunnelUsage`/`useUsageSummary` hooks (Step 5a) are plain `useQuery`, zero mutations. No speculative code path preparing for usage-driven billing was found on either side.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/subscriptionPlanLimitService.test.ts` — FREE/PRO/ENTERPRISE accounts each get their real plan's limits; most-recent-Subscription-row selection verified; no-Subscription-row defaults to FREE; PAST_DUE keeps the real plan; SUSPENDED/RESTRICTED/CANCELED/DELETED all downgrade to FREE even on a PRO subscription (and skip the subscription lookup entirely); missing Account row defaults to FREE.
**Status:** active — context.md risks #6 and #36 marked FIXED; item 41 added for the newly-discovered `GracePeriodWorker` scheduling gap

### 2026-09-14 — Schedule GracePeriodWorker: matched apiKeyPlugin.ts's instantiate/schedule/onClose shape, used the class's own self-managing start()/stop() rather than a redundant external interval

**Decided by:** Claude Code (reliability-gaps session)
**Context:** context.md item 41 — `GracePeriodWorker` (moves a `PAST_DUE` account to `SUSPENDED` once its grace period expires) was fully written but never instantiated or scheduled anywhere.
**Investigation:** Read `apiKeyPlugin.ts`'s worker section to find the established scheduling pattern before inventing anything: `FlushUsageWorker`/`ExpireRotationGraceWorker`/`ExpireApiKeysWorker` are all plain `run()`-only classes with an *externally*-managed `setInterval` in the plugin, cleaned up via `fastify.addHook('onClose', ...)`. `GracePeriodWorker` is architecturally different from those three — it already has its own complete, self-contained `start()`/`stop()` public API (`start()` runs an immediate sweep, then a `setInterval` internally; `.tick()`, the actual sweep logic, is private). Matching the other three workers' pattern literally (wrapping an external `setInterval` around a public `tick()`) isn't possible without either exposing a private method or duplicating the interval logic `start()` already does correctly.
**Decision:** Match the *shape* of the existing pattern (instantiate → schedule → clean up on `onClose`) rather than its exact mechanics, since `GracePeriodWorker`'s own encapsulation already provides the scheduling half. Registered it in `billing.plugin.ts` (its own home module — also where its own header comment already said to register it, unlike the other three which live in and are scheduled by `apiKeyPlugin.ts`), calling `.start()` once and `.stop()` in `billing.plugin.ts`'s own `onClose` hook (a hook this plugin didn't previously have — added it).
**"Stale accounts on first deploy" question, decided explicitly:** Does this fix's first run need to specially sweep accounts whose `graceEndsAt` already passed before this fix ever shipped, or is it safe to let the normal hourly interval catch them? Re-read `GracePeriodWorker.start()`'s own implementation before deciding: `this.tick().catch(console.error); this.timer = setInterval(() => this.tick()..., 60*60*1000)` — it already runs one sweep *immediately* on start, before ever waiting for the first hourly tick. This means "first deploy of this fix" is not a special case at all — it behaves identically to any other restart, and any restart already sweeps immediately. No backfill script, no special first-run flag, no migration needed: the moment this code ships and the server restarts, every already-stale `PAST_DUE` account gets caught within that immediate sweep, not up to an hour later.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/gracePeriodWorker.test.ts` — calls the class's private `tick()` directly via a cast (same convention this suite already uses elsewhere for a class whose public API is deliberately self-managing, e.g. `LocalAgentClient`'s test poking `cachedAgent`), plus one test specifically exercising `start()` itself (not just `tick()`) to lock in the "immediate sweep on start" behavior the first-deploy answer above depends on.
**Status:** active — context.md item 41 marked FIXED

### 2026-09-14 — onAgentClose cross-connection race, real fix: per-key mutex + compare-and-delete, not a bigger synchronization primitive

**Decided by:** Claude Code (reliability-gaps session)
**Context:** context.md risk #19 (remaining half) / decision.md's 2026-09-12 "onAgentClose" entry: awaiting `router.onAgentClose(ws)` inside the WS close handler was a real correctness/observability improvement but explicitly did not eliminate the actual race — `onAgentClose`'s async `SubdomainRegistry.unregister()` and a reconnecting agent's async `SubdomainRegistry.register()` for the same label run on two independent WS connections with no ordering guarantee between their Redis round-trips.
**Investigation before designing anything:** Read the full call path on both sides. `AgentRegistry.register()`/`.evict()` are pure synchronous in-memory `Map` operations with no `await` inside them — two synchronous calls can never interleave with each other at the JS level, so the in-memory registry was never actually at risk; the entire race lives in `SubdomainRegistry`'s two async Redis calls. Also found `SubdomainRegistry.unregister()` was a **blind delete** — `redis.del(key)` with no check that the current value at that key still belonged to the agent that's disconnecting. That, not just missing synchronization, is the root cause: whichever of `register()`'s `SET` or `unregister()`'s `DEL` happens to land *last* at the Redis level wins, regardless of which was logically supposed to happen "first." A second, independent instance of the identical blind-delete pattern was found in `HttpTunnelHandler`'s stale-Redis-entry cleanup path (`if (!agent) { await this.subdomainRegistry.unregister(label, accountSlug); ... }`) — same bug, same fix applies.
**Decision — combine a per-key mutex with a compare-and-delete, not a Lua/Redis-level atomic script:** Considered an atomic Redis-side fix (a Lua `EVAL` script doing GET+compare+DEL in one round-trip, which `@upstash/redis`'s client does support via `.eval()`) versus an in-process per-(label,accountSlug) async mutex. Chose the in-process mutex because the actual deployed concurrency model is single-instance — `HubPubSub`/multi-hub is a confirmed no-op stub (decision.md, 2026-09-12, "HubPubSub stub") — so there is no cross-process concurrency to guard against, only cross-connection concurrency *within* one Hub process, which a simple in-memory lock fully and correctly serializes without introducing Lua scripting (a new, more exotic primitive this codebase doesn't otherwise use) for a problem that doesn't require distributed atomicity. The mutex alone was verified insufficient on its own, though: a naive "whichever call enters the queue first wins" lock does not by itself guarantee the *newer* registration survives — if the old connection's slow unregister happens to be queued first, it would still correctly delete-then-let-the-new-SET-through, but if timing meant the check inside unregister happened without an identity check, an old unregister queued *after* a fresh register could still wipe it. The **compare-and-delete** (`unregister()` now takes `expectedAgentId` and only deletes if the current Redis value's `agentId` still matches) is what actually makes the outcome correct *regardless of ordering* — the mutex's job is only to prevent the compare-and-delete's own GET-then-DEL from being interleaved by another operation on the same key, not to decide who "wins."
**Both real callers updated**, not just the one named in the bug report: `Message.router.ts`'s `onAgentClose` (passes `session.agentId`) and `HttpTunnelHandler`'s stale-entry cleanup (passes the resolved entry's own `agentId`) — found while reading `unregister()`'s call sites before changing its signature, not assumed to be the only caller.
**Verified the tests actually discriminate old vs. new**, not just pass against the new code: temporarily restored the pre-fix `SubdomainRegistry` (blind delete, no lock) and re-ran the new test file against it — 3 of 5 tests failed as expected (the stale-agentId no-op test, the slow-unregister-vs-fast-register ordering test, and the mutex-concurrency test); the other 2 (plain disconnect, different-labels-not-serialized) correctly still passed since they don't exercise the race. Restored the fix afterward and confirmed all 5 pass.
**Tested:** `tests/e2e/subdomainRegistryRace.test.ts` — plain disconnect; a stale unregister (wrong agentId) never deletes a newer registration, with zero timing involved; a slow unregister called first doesn't delete a faster new registration that lands while it's in flight; the mutex provably prevents two operations on the same key from ever running concurrently; different labels are confirmed *not* serialized against each other (no unnecessary global lock).
**Status:** active — context.md risk #19 marked fully FIXED

### 2026-09-14 — Zombie ts-node-dev processes: a predev guard script, not a process-manager swap

**Decided by:** Claude Code (reliability-gaps session)
**Context:** context.md risk #35 — stopping `apps/api`'s dev server by killing only the port-9000 listener leaves the `ts-node-dev --respawn` supervisor alive, which silently respawns later. Fixed only via documentation (`LOCAL_DEV_BACKEND.md`'s corrected stop procedure) — nothing enforced it.
**Options considered:** (1) swap `ts-node-dev` for a different dev-server process model that doesn't use a supervisor/worker split (e.g. `tsx watch`, already a devDependency in this package for seed scripts) — rejected without a full investigation into `tsx watch`'s own process model, since swapping the actual dev-server tooling is a materially bigger, riskier change (behavior differences across the whole dev workflow) than this reliability gap warranted, and there was no confirmation `tsx watch` doesn't have its own version of a supervisor/child split. (2) A `predev` guard script that cleans up any leftover instance before every `pnpm dev` — chosen, since this is dev-only tooling (not production code) and the actual goal — stop zombies from *accumulating* — doesn't require the underlying tool to never leave orphans, only that the next start always cleans up first.
**Implementation:** `apps/api/scripts/kill-zombie-dev.sh` (matching the existing `scripts/*.sh` convention already used for `create-module.sh`), wired as `"predev": "bash scripts/kill-zombie-dev.sh"` in `apps/api/package.json` — pnpm/npm run `pre<script>` hooks automatically before the named script, no extra invocation needed anywhere `pnpm dev` is already called.
**Process-matching pattern, decided carefully, not copied blindly from the existing docs:** Empirically ran a real `pnpm dev` instance and inspected `ps aux` directly rather than assuming the previously-documented grep pattern was precise. Found the supervisor's command line includes `apps/api/node_modules/...ts-node-dev/lib/bin.js`, but the worker's does **not** contain `apps/api` at all (it resolves through the monorepo root's `node_modules/.pnpm` store: `.../Black-Server/node_modules/.pnpm/ts-node-dev@.../lib/wrap.js`) — so matching on `apps/api/src/server.ts` (as `LOCAL_DEV_BACKEND.md`'s manual command partly does) would silently miss the worker process entirely. Matching on bare `ts-node-dev` alone, on the other hand, is too broad for an *automated, unattended* script (unlike a manual command a human reviews before running) — it would also match a `ts-node-dev` process from a completely unrelated project on the same machine. Settled on requiring **both** `Black-Server` (this repo's own on-disk directory name, present in both processes' full paths) **and** `ts-node-dev` to appear in the command line (`ps aux | grep "Black-Server" | grep "ts-node-dev"`) — precise enough to never touch an unrelated project's process or this repo's own non-ts-node-dev processes (e.g. `apps/web`'s Next.js dev server), broad enough to catch both the supervisor and the worker regardless of which `node_modules` resolution path pnpm happens to use.
**Verified for real** (this is OS process management, not application logic — a manual, actually-performed verification is the right check here, not a unit test): ran the real local dev server on this machine through three full cycles of start → stop-the-wrong-way (`lsof -ti :9000 | xargs kill -9`, killing only the port listener) → restart. Confirmed via `ps aux | grep ts-node-dev` after each wrong-stop that the zombie supervisor genuinely survived (reproducing the original bug), and confirmed after each restart that the `predev` script found and force-killed it before the new instance started, leaving exactly one clean supervisor+worker pair each time. Final state after all three cycles confirmed empty via both `lsof -ti :9000` and `ps aux | grep ts-node-dev`.
**Status:** active — context.md risk #35 marked FIXED

### 2026-09-14 — Step 6 ("admin-only screens") closed out with no migration work: the premise was false

**Decided by:** joint (Claude Code investigation, confirmed and directed by Tanveer)
**Context:** decision.md's 2026-09-09 "Frontend migration sequencing" entry scheduled admin-only screens as Step 6, the last step of the VhyxUI migration, assuming (never verified until this session) that real MUI admin screens existed and needed restyling — same as every prior Step 5x screen. This session's brief explicitly required verifying scope by reading files directly before migrating anything, per the standing pattern that every prior step found real discrepancies.
**Investigation findings:**
- `apps/web/src/views/admin/` contained 6 files (`AdminUsersTables.tsx`, `AdminForm.tsx`, `RoleTable.tsx`, `RoleDialog.tsx`, `AbilityTables.tsx`, `AbilityDialog.tsx`) — all confirmed **100% commented-out** (zero real, non-comment lines in any of them; verified with `grep -vcE '^\s*(//|$)'` returning 0 for each), containing only mock/hardcoded data (`mockAdminUsers`), never a real API call.
- Zero route under `app/` referenced any of them (`grep -rn "views/admin" app/` → empty). Zero other file in `apps/web/src` imported any of them (only self-references, all commented).
- No admin login page, no admin API client/service/hooks anywhere in `apps/web/src/api`, no middleware admin-route guard (`apps/web/src/middleware.ts` doesn't exist).
- The backend's Admin RBAC system (`apps/api`, `/api/v1/admin/identity/*`) is, by contrast, fully real and complete — separate admin login/session, admin-user/role/ability CRUD, `requireAbility`-gated routes, immutable `AdminAuditLog` on every mutation. It has simply never had a frontend built against it.
- The original gap-analysis report's single flagged Autocomplete usage (cited in this step's brief as "admin-only, the single lowest-priority item blocking full MUI removal") lived only inside the dead `AbilityDialog.tsx` scaffold (a commented-out `CreatableAutocomplete` import, intended for an ability's `category` field — a creatable single-select, not even a true multi-select). Grepped the entire app for real, non-commented Autocomplete usage: `apps/web/src/libs/components/CreatableAutocomplete.tsx` and `apps/web/src/@core/components/mui/Autocomplete.tsx` both exist as real code but have **zero live importers** anywhere. There is no live Autocomplete/multi-select usage anywhere in the app today.
- Checked VhyxUI directly (sibling repo, currently linked at 0.3.1-alpha, matching `apps/web/node_modules/@vhyxui/react`'s resolved version): `packages/react/src/components/` still has no Combobox/Multiselect/Autocomplete component (the one `role: 'combobox'` hit in `Select.tsx` is a standard ARIA attribute on a single-select trigger, not a multi-select primitive). Gap confirmed still current, not stale — but moot for this project today given no live usage exists to migrate.
**Options considered:** (1) build a real admin frontend from scratch in VhyxUI now, treating this as net-new feature work rather than a migration step; (2) document the discrepancy and close out the VhyxUI migration as complete after Steps 1-5, since there is no existing admin UI to migrate; (3) same as (2), plus delete the dead scaffold as confirmed-abandoned cleanup.
**Decision:** Option 3 — documented and closed out (no new admin UI built this session; that's a separate, much larger, explicitly-deferred product decision), and the 6 dead scaffold files were deleted (`apps/web/src/views/admin/` removed entirely) after re-confirming zero references immediately before deletion.
**Rationale:** Migrating restyling effort into files that were never real, never routed, and never functional would have been pure waste — there was nothing MUI to "migrate," since the scaffold never worked even as MUI. Building a real admin frontend is legitimate future work but is categorically different from a "restyle existing screens" migration step (new login flow, new API client, new CRUD screens against a real backend) and deserves its own scoping/planning session rather than being folded silently into a migration closeout. Deleting the dead scaffold (rather than leaving it) was chosen because it was confirmed unreferenced and confirmed to represent an abandoned first attempt, not a work-in-progress worth preserving as a starting point — a real future admin-frontend session should design against the actual current backend contract, not resume from stale mock-data scaffolding.
**Status:** active. **The VhyxUI migration (Steps 0-5) is now considered complete.** See context.md's new "Missing Features" section for the admin-frontend gap, tracked there as a product gap, not a migration debt item.

### 2026-09-15 — NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY removal: client-side request signing dropped for apps/web dashboard traffic

**Decided by:** joint (directed by Tanveer, following up on `TABLE_API_ARCHITECTURE_COMPARISON.md`'s convergence point #6)
**Context:** The cross-project comparison report found `NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY` shipped to the browser bundle in all three codebases (VhyxVoid, kautilyan-admin, kautilyan-frontend) — readable by anyone, providing no real confidentiality, while adding real maintenance cost (an independent HMAC implementation to keep in sync) and drift risk. This session investigated and acted on VhyxVoid's side only.
**Investigation (required before touching anything, per the brief):**
1. **What did the signing actually protect?** Read `apps/web/src/api/wrapper/http.ts`'s `coreFetch()` fully: every request (public and authenticated) got `x-api-key`/`x-nonce`/`x-timestamp`/`x-signature` headers, HMAC-SHA256 over a canonical payload string, keyed by `NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_API_KEY`. Traced the other side: `apps/api`'s only signature-checking code, `signatureVerification` (`core/utils/auth.util.ts`), is a real, correctly-written implementation (nonce replay protection via Redis, timestamp window, constant-time compare) — but grepping for importers found **zero**, anywhere outside its own definition file. It is never registered as a `preHandler`/`onRequest` hook on any route. Even hypothetically wired, its one hardcoded client lookup (`getClientSecret`: `client_123` → `CLIENT_123_SECRET`, a literal placeholder value in `.env`, `your_client_123_secret_here`) does not correspond to `apps/web`'s real `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_KEY` (`blackserver-api-key-12345`) at all — these were never wired to match, even in intent. **Conclusion: the signing protected nothing, verified by nothing, for the entire time it's existed.**
2. **What's the real auth mechanism, and does any endpoint rely on the HMAC signature as its only protection?** Traced `userAuthGuard.ts` (the guard actually used by every protected route — account, identity, tunnel, notification, feedback, apiKey, billing, confirmed via grep across all real route files): it reads only `request.headers.authorization`, verifies via `RS256JwtService.verify()` (asymmetric, real key pair). **This directly contradicted context.md's own documented Core Flows §5 diagram**, which described a cookie-based flow (`Api-->>User: ... set access_token cookie`, `GET /account/... (Cookie: access_token)`). Checked exhaustively: `apps/web` never sets an `access_token` cookie anywhere (grepped `access_token` across the whole frontend — the only hit was a dead, commented-out line in the now-deleted legacy `fetchData.ts`); `apps/api` never `Set-Cookie`s it either (grepped every `setCookie` call in the backend — exactly one, for the *refresh* token). A second, cookie-based JWT guard does exist (`core/middleware/auth.middleware.ts`'s `authenticate`, using Fastify's generic `req.jwtVerify()` against the `access_token` cookie name `@fastify/jwt` is configured with) — but it has zero importers anywhere, fully dead, never wired onto any route. **Conclusion: real auth is Bearer-JWT-in-header, not cookie-based as context.md previously (incorrectly) documented; no endpoint depends on the HMAC signature for anything — safe to remove.** context.md's Core Flows §5 corrected in the same pass (see context.md item 42 and the flow diagram itself).
3. **CSRF.** Since the real session credential travels via an explicit `Authorization` header rather than an ambient cookie, the vast majority of endpoints have no CSRF surface regardless of signing — a cross-site page cannot make a victim's browser attach a header it has no access to. The one cookie in the entire flow (the refresh token) is already `httpOnly`, `sameSite: 'strict'`, `path: '/api/v1/auth'` — real, correctly-configured, pre-existing CSRF protection, confirmed by reading `setRefreshCookie()` directly rather than assumed. **Conclusion: no CSRF gap exists; no new code needed.**
4. **Rate limiting on session-authenticated endpoints.** `register.plugin.ts` registers `@fastify/rate-limit` globally (`{max: 100, timeWindow: '1 minute'}`) directly on the root Fastify instance — Fastify hooks added at root apply to every route in that encapsulation context regardless of registration order relative to route registration (hooks resolve at `.ready()`, not at registration time). **Conclusion: real, if blunt (not per-plan/per-account like the gateway path's `ValidateApiKeyUseCase`), rate limiting already exists; no urgent gap, flagged only as a possible future refinement, not built this session.**
**Options considered:** (1) leave signing in place, just stop shipping `NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY` (breaks every request — not viable); (2) move signing server-side somehow (pointless — the thing being "protected" is calls the browser itself is making with a user's own real session, there's no third party to authenticate against); (3) remove client-side signing entirely, since the real, working protection (`userAuthGuard`) is independent of it and already sufficient.
**Decision:** Option 3. Implemented:
- `apps/web/src/api/wrapper/http.ts`: `coreFetch()` no longer builds or sends `x-api-key`/`x-nonce`/`x-timestamp`/`x-signature`; the `generateNonce`/`generateTimestamp`/`generateSignature`/`preparePayload` imports and the credentials-missing throw are gone. Also deleted ~130 lines of dead, triple-duplicated commented-out prior-draft implementations at the bottom of the file while it was already being rewritten (302 → ~150 lines — smaller, not just changed, per the brief's explicit instruction).
- `apps/web/src/api/hooks/signer.ts` deleted (zero importers once `http.ts` stopped using it); the now-empty `api/hooks/` directory removed too.
- `apps/web/.env`: `NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_API_KEY` removed (confirmed via grep this was their only real usage; `apps/web/.env.local` never had them).
- `apps/web/src/libs/components/Confirmation.tsx`: the dead default-action path (`apiUrl`/`method`/`payloadData` shorthand, calling a **second, independent** HTTP client) removed, along with the now-orphaned `redirectPath`/`successFeedbackMessage`/`useRouter` import/`ApiResponse`/`isApiResponse` helpers and the local `HttpMethod` type export (confirmed zero external importers of any of these before removing). Every real call site already used `onConfirm`, confirmed via `grep -rn "apiUrl="` across `apps/web/src` → empty, matching `TABLE_API_ARCHITECTURE_COMPARISON.md`'s claim, re-verified directly rather than trusted.
- `apps/web/src/utils/fetchData.ts` deleted (its own independent, from-scratch HMAC signing implementation — worse than `http.ts`'s: a hardcoded `'x-api-key': 'livein-key'` header that didn't even read the real API key env var for that header despite computing the signature with it, and a stray `console.log` of the secret). `apps/web/src/utils/convertFormdataInObject.ts` deleted too — its only two callers (`fetchData.ts`, `signer.ts`) were both just removed.
- `crypto-js`/`@types/crypto-js` removed from `apps/web/package.json` (now-unused — both prior callers were `fetchData.ts` and `signer.ts`); `pnpm install` re-run to keep the lockfile consistent.
**A real, independent bug found and fixed while rewriting `Confirmation.tsx`'s `handleConfirm`**: in the original code, `onSuccessCallback?.()` was only ever called from inside `handleDefaultAction()` — meaning for every real call site (100% of them use `onConfirm`, not the dead default path), `onSuccessCallback` silently never fired. `TableAction.tsx`'s `BulkActions` has always passed `onSuccessCallback={() => clearSelection()}` alongside a real `onConfirm` — so bulk-action selection was never actually being cleared after a successful confirm, previously invisible since `TableAction.tsx`/`BulkActions` itself was separately confirmed to have zero live importers anywhere in the app (a leftover from the deleted admin scaffold — see the 2026-09-14 Step 6 closeout entry — so this bug had no live user-facing effect, but the fix is correct and now in place for whenever this component gets a real consumer again). Fixed by calling `onSuccessCallback?.()` unconditionally after a successful `onConfirm()`.
**Rationale:** The signing layer was pure overhead with a documented security report specifically calling it out as convergent, unexamined risk across three codebases — removing it here both resolves the report's finding and simplifies the client, exactly as instructed. Confirming (rather than assuming) that the real auth mechanism was independently sufficient, that CSRF was already handled, and that rate limiting already existed meant this could be a pure subtraction with no compensating new code required — a better outcome than the brief's own framing anticipated (which expected CSRF might need new work).
**Verified against the real local dev backend, not just reasoned about**: `apps/api` started against the local Postgres test DB per `LOCAL_DEV_BACKEND.md`; a real `POST /auth/login` (zero signature headers sent) returned a valid `accessToken`; `GET /account/me` with only `Authorization: Bearer <token>` (no signature headers) returned `200`; the same route with no token returned `401`; a disallowed `Origin` was still rejected by CORS (confirms cross-origin protection is unaffected by this change). The browser extension needed for a full UI click-through wasn't connected in this environment — the HTTP-level verification above directly exercises the exact layer that changed (the request/auth path), which is the relevant coverage for this specific change.
**New test**: `tests/e2e/userAuthGuard.test.ts` (5 tests) — calls the `userAuthGuard` plugin function directly against a real `RS256JwtService` backed by a freshly-generated RSA keypair (valid token succeeds and populates `request.user`; missing header, missing `Bearer` prefix, tampered token, and a token signed by a different keypair all correctly reject). Deliberately does **not** boot a real Fastify server: `fastify` itself isn't resolvable from a bare import under `tests/e2e/` given this repo's pnpm-isolated `node_modules` layout (it lives only in `apps/api/node_modules`, not hoisted to root, and this suite's existing tests are run via a root-level `vitest` — confirmed by attempting the import and getting `Cannot find package 'fastify'`). Adding `fastify` as a duplicate root devDependency just for one test risked drifting from `apps/api`'s real installed version over time; calling the plugin function directly (it's exactly what `fastify-plugin`'s `fp()` wrapper returns unchanged) exercises the identical guard logic without either problem, and matches this suite's existing pure-function testing convention (`internalProxyAuth.test.ts`).
**New findings, flagged, not acted on this session (both explicitly out of this session's scope):**
- `signatureVerification` and `core/middleware/auth.middleware.ts`'s `authenticate` are both confirmed fully dead backend code (zero importers), along with the placeholder `CLIENT_123_SECRET` env var — a backend dead-code cleanup, not touched here since this session's authorized scope was `apps/web`. See context.md item 42.
- kautilyan-admin and kautilyan-frontend share the identical `NEXT_PUBLIC_SECRET_KEY`-exposure pattern (per the comparison report) and need the same investigate-then-remove treatment applied separately in their own repos/sessions — their own backends' signature-checking wiring was not investigated here and should not be assumed dead just because `apps/api`'s was. See context.md item 43.
**Status:** active. context.md's Core Flows §5 diagram corrected in the same pass (was describing a cookie-based auth flow that doesn't match the real code — a genuine pre-existing documentation error, not introduced by this change, just found while investigating it).

### 2026-09-15 — Phase 1 adoption: @vhyx/api-kit in VhyxVoid

**Decided by:** joint (directed by Tanveer, implementing `TABLE_API_ARCHITECTURE_COMPARISON.md`'s Part 3 Phase 1 recommendation)
**Context:** The comparison report recommended extracting the converged API-client/query-key layer (shared across VhyxVoid, kautilyan-admin, kautilyan-frontend) into a shared package now, while deferring the table-component fix/extraction (Phase 2) until proven in place first. This session implemented Phase 1: create the package, adopt it in VhyxVoid only.
**Options considered (repo structure):** (1) fold the shared code into VhyxVoid's own monorepo as a workspace package — rejected, since the whole point is sharing across kautilyan-admin/kautilyan-frontend too, which aren't part of this monorepo and shouldn't need to depend on it; (2) publish to a private/local npm registry — rejected for the same reason VhyxUI rejected it (see 2026-09-09 entry): registry-publish overhead per iteration during the phase where this package is still being proven out across real consumers; (3) a new, separate sibling repo, consumed via pnpm `link:`, mirroring VhyxUI's exact precedent.
**Decision:** Option 3. New repo `vhyx-api-kit` at `/Users/tanveer/Documents/tanveer/vhyx-api-kit`, sibling to `Black-Server` and `VhyxUI`. Package name `@vhyx/api-kit` — deliberately not `@vhyxvoid/*` (would misrepresent it as VhyxVoid-owned when its purpose is cross-project sharing, same reasoning VhyxUI's own `@vhyxui/*` scope was chosen for) and not literally `@vhyxui/*` either (different product, would be confusing). `vhyx-` as a prefix matches this user's own established personal-project naming convention observed directly in the sibling directory (`vhyxChart`, `vhyxai`, `vhyxstore`, `vhyxara-academy`, etc. — "Vhyx" is a personal/umbrella brand across many of this user's projects, not something specific to VhyxVoid or VhyxUI), so it fits the existing neighborhood rather than introducing a new naming scheme.
**Package design, decided carefully per the brief's explicit instruction not to hardcode VhyxVoid-only assumptions:**
- `createQueryKeys(base)` extracted with identical behavior to VhyxVoid's own `utils/utility.ts` version (confirmed byte-for-byte identical to kautilyan-admin's copy per the comparison report) — the one piece with zero design ambiguity.
- `createHttpClient(config)`: the transport mechanics (body/FormData handling, envelope unwrapping, `ApiError` typing) are owned by the package; auth-header attachment (`getAuthHeaders`) and unauthorized-response handling (`onUnauthorized`, receiving a `retry()` callback) are injected per project. Deliberately does NOT implement a refresh-queue, redirect-with-dedupe, or any other concrete auth *policy* inside the package itself — those differ genuinely across the three source projects (VhyxVoid: client-side refresh queue; kautilyan-admin: force-redirect with a dedupe guard; kautilyan-frontend: none, refresh is server-side) and belong in each project's own auth state, not baked into shared transport code.
- `createQueryClient(config)`: reconstructs kautilyan-admin's documented onError/toast/retry/session-death behavior. This session did not have kautilyan-admin's actual source — the comparison report's own investigation (an independent research pass, not this session) is the only source of truth available, and it was read carefully rather than guessed past. Where the report was precise (toast-on-error, a session-death hook with a dedupe guard owned by the consumer, skip-retry-on-auth-errors), the factory matches it. Where it wasn't precise enough to be certain (the exact retry count — VhyxVoid's own prior code used 2, the report says kautilyan-admin's was "up to 3"), a reasonable default (2, matching VhyxVoid's own prior value) was chosen and left overridable via the `retry` option, documented in the code as a default-not-a-verbatim-copy rather than silently presented as more precisely sourced than it is. No UI/toast-library dependency of the package's own — `onNotify` is a plain callback, since VhyxVoid and kautilyan-admin turned out to use genuinely different toast libraries even between themselves (discovered mid-session, see below) and hardcoding one would have been wrong for at least one consumer.
- Ships compiled `dist/` (plain `tsc`, not a bundler — this package has no CSS/JSX to bundle, unlike VhyxUI, so `tsc` alone is sufficient and matches the "keep this as simple as the actual scope warrants" instruction) rather than raw TS source, matching VhyxUI's own established precedent rather than introducing a new, unproven `transpilePackages`-based mechanism.
- 31 tests from day one (query-key shape, every `httpClient` behavior including the unauthorized-retry hook and FormData/envelope edge cases, every `queryClient` behavior including the session-death/skip-notify interaction) — committed as part of the repo's very first commit, not accumulated as later debt the way VhyxVoid's own test coverage did.
**VhyxVoid adoption, mechanical but with one real design constraint**: `api/types/api.ts`'s local `ApiError` class was replaced with a **re-export** of the shared package's, not left as a second, separately-defined class — `error instanceof ApiError` checks throughout the app (in `queryClient.ts`'s `skipNotify`, and anywhere else that pattern is used) must refer to the exact class the shared `httpClient` actually throws, or every such check would silently and permanently fail. Confirmed via grep this class was only referenced from the three files already being rewritten (`http.ts`, `queryClient.ts`, `api.ts` itself) — no other call site needed updating.
**A real, independent finding surfaced while wiring the toast fix, corrects the prior record**: item 42 (and `LOCAL_DEV_BACKEND.md`, until this session) described VhyxVoid's dead toast pipeline as "no `<ToastContainer/>` mounted anywhere in the app." Investigated directly (not re-derived from the old description) while wiring the new `createQueryClient`'s `onNotify` and found this was imprecise: `apps/web/src/api/provider/providers.client.tsx` has always mounted a real, working `<Toaster/>` — from `react-hot-toast`. The actual bug was that `queryClient.ts` called `toast.error(...)` from a **second**, separately-installed toast library, `react-toastify`, whose own `<ToastContainer/>` genuinely was never mounted anywhere. `utils/copyToClipboard.ts` had the identical bug (same wrong import), found by grepping every `react-hot-toast`/`react-toastify` import in the app before assuming the fix's scope was only `queryClient.ts`.
**Decision on the toast fix**: wire onto `react-hot-toast` (the library actually mounted) rather than adding a second, competing `<ToastContainer/>` for `react-toastify` alongside the existing `<Toaster/>`. Running two toast systems simultaneously would be visually inconsistent (different animation/positioning defaults) and pure redundancy — better to consolidate onto the one that already works. `copyToClipboard.ts` fixed the same way in the same pass, since it's the identical bug and the fix is a one-line import swap.
**Verified**: `pnpm --filter @vhyxvoid/web typecheck` and `build` both pass clean (19 routes, unchanged); `vhyx-api-kit`'s own 31 tests pass; against the real local dev backend (`LOCAL_DEV_BACKEND.md` convention), a real login (zero signature headers, confirming the previous session's fix is undisturbed) and a real failing request both confirmed the `{success:false,message,...}` → `ApiError` → `onNotify` chain is wired correctly end-to-end at the data level. **Not independently confirmed**: the final DOM-visual "a toast actually renders" step — the Claude in Chrome browser extension was not connected in this environment (same limitation the 2026-09-15 signing-removal session hit). Relied on `react-hot-toast`'s own well-established behavior (a global singleton store; calling `toast.error()` anywhere renders into any mounted `<Toaster/>`, no ancestor/context relationship required) rather than a screenshot. Flagged here rather than silently claimed as fully verified.
**Explicitly not done this session, per the report's own sequencing and this session's explicit brief**: kautilyan-admin and kautilyan-frontend were not touched — they need their own separate adoption sessions once this package is proven out here first. The table component (`GenericServerTable`)'s internal-query-ownership flaw (Phase 2 of the report's recommendation) was not touched — this session was Phase 1 only.
**Status:** active.

### 2026-09-15 — Phase 2 pilot: Members converted to props-based table pattern

**Decided by:** Claude Code (Phase 2 pilot session)
**Context:** `TABLE_API_ARCHITECTURE_COMPARISON.md` Part 3 recommended converting `GenericServerTable` from a self-fetching component (internal `useQuery` keyed by an ad hoc `[tableKey, params]` string) to a props-based one (`data`/`isLoading`/`error`/`total` as props), proven on one table first — Members, since it already carries the Step 5c/closing-check point-fix (a second `invalidateQueries` call matching the table's ad hoc key) that this structural fix should make genuinely removable.
**Options considered:** (1) convert `GenericServerTable` itself and touch every consumer to match; (2) add a parallel props-based component and migrate tables to it one at a time, leaving the old component alone; (3) make `GenericServerTable` support both a legacy `tableKey`+`fetchData` mode and a new props mode simultaneously (dual-mode).
**Decision:** Option 1, with a compatibility shim for not-yet-converted consumers rather than a dual-mode component. `GenericServerTable` now *only* accepts the props-based contract (`serverTable`, `data`, `isLoading`, `error`, `total`, `extra`) — no more `tableKey`/`fetchData`. A new hook, `libs/table/useSelfFetchingServerTable.ts`, reproduces the exact old internal-query behavior (same key shape, same staleness risk, same reliance on each table's own point-fix) for every consumer not being converted this session.
**Rationale:** Option 2 (parallel component) would leave two table implementations to maintain and two places for the click-to-sort/props-contract work to happen — real duplication for a "prove it on one table" pilot that's explicitly meant to become the only pattern. Option 3 (dual-mode) was tempting since it would have required zero changes to Tunnels/API Keys, but a component silently branching its entire data-ownership model on which props are passed is a worse shared-infra shape than a single contract plus an explicitly-named, well-commented compat shim at each unconverted call site — the shim makes "this table hasn't been converted yet" visible in the caller's own code, where a dual-mode component would hide it inside `GenericServerTable` itself. The shim is intentionally named and commented to discourage copying it for a *new* table.
**Consumers found beyond the report's inventory:** grepping for `GenericServerTable` usage (not trusting the report's "3 real tables" count) found two more direct consumers the report never inventoried — `InvitationsTab.tsx` and `MyAccountsTable.tsx` — plus `FeedbackHistoryTab.tsx`. All three now use the same compat shim as Tunnels/API Keys; none were converted or otherwise touched architecturally.
**Status:** active. GenericServerTable's contract change is permanent (not reverting to self-fetching); API Keys/Tunnels/Invitations/My-Organizations/My-Feedback remain on the compat shim until their own conversion sessions.

### 2026-09-15 — Phase 2 pilot: point-fix removal, verified not just deleted

**Decided by:** Claude Code (Phase 2 pilot session)
**Context:** The task brief's explicit "concrete proof step" — once Members' table query is keyed through `memberKeys.list({accountId, ...params})` (the same `createQueryKeys` factory every mutation hook already invalidates against) instead of an ad hoc `[tableKey, params]` string, the Step 5c/closing-check session's second `invalidateQueries({queryKey: ['members-${accountId}']})` calls in `useChangeMemberRole`/`useRemoveMember`/`useTransferOwnership` become dead code.
**Options considered:** (1) remove the point-fix calls and trust the structural argument; (2) remove them and verify with a live functional test only (repeat the Step 5c browser test); (3) remove them and add an automated test asserting the actual React Query partial-key-match behavior the fix depends on, in addition to a live check.
**Decision:** Option 3. Added `api/infrastructure/query-keys/account.keys.test.ts` — seeds a real `QueryClient` with a cache entry under the table's real parameterized key and confirms `invalidateQueries({queryKey: memberKeys.list({accountId})})` (the exact call the mutation hooks make) marks it invalidated, plus a negative case confirming a different `accountId`'s entry is untouched. Also ran the live check (PATCH `alicesss`'s role on Acme Corp, re-GET the exact query the table runs, confirmed the change visible, reverted).
**Rationale:** A live functional check alone (option 2) proves the specific manual scenario worked once, in this sandbox, today — it doesn't protect against a future refactor silently reintroducing the disjoint-key bug (e.g. someone changing `memberKeys`'s shape or the table's param set). The automated test encodes the actual mechanism (React Query's default partial-key matching) as a permanent regression guard, which is a stronger and more durable form of "verified working after the fix" than the brief's own literal instruction (repeat the browser test) would have produced alone — the browser test was also not fully available this session (see the Part 0 carry-over note in context.md item 45).
**Status:** active

### 2026-09-15 — Phase 2 pilot: useInviteMember/useCancelInvitation deliberately not touched, despite being named in the brief

**Decided by:** Claude Code (Phase 2 pilot session) — a deviation from the literal task instruction, made transparently
**Context:** The task brief listed `useInviteMember`/`useCancelInvitation` alongside `useChangeMemberRole`/`useRemoveMember`/`useTransferOwnership` as hooks needing their "second invalidateQueries call" removed. Investigating each: `useInviteMember`'s ad hoc invalidation targets `invitations-${accountId}` (the Invitations tab's table key) — inviting someone creates a pending invitation, not an immediate Members-table row, so this hook never had a `members-${accountId}` invalidate to remove in the first place. `useCancelInvitation` (in `useOrg.ts`, a different file than the brief implied) has the identical shape, also targeting `invitations-${accountId}`. `InvitationsTab.tsx` — confirmed via grep to be rendered from `OrgSettingsView`, not the Members page — was not converted to the props-based pattern this session (it's on the `useSelfFetchingServerTable` compat shim, still self-fetching via its own ad hoc key).
**Options considered:** (1) follow the brief literally and remove both hooks' invalidation calls; (2) investigate first, and leave both untouched since their target table (Invitations) wasn't converted.
**Decision:** Option 2. Removing either would have broken `InvitationsTab`'s only working invalidation path, since that table still relies on the exact ad hoc-key-matching workaround these calls provide.
**Rationale:** The brief's own reasoning for removing the point-fix was "once the structural fix lands, the second invalidateQueries call becomes genuinely removable dead code" — that precondition (the structural fix landing) was true for Members' three hooks but never true for Invitations, which this session's scope (Members only, per the brief's own "prove the pattern on one table first" instruction) never touched. Following the literal instruction here would have reintroduced Invitations' version of the exact bug this whole migration is trying to eliminate.
**Status:** active — revisit when Invitations gets its own Phase 2 conversion session; at that point these two calls become the removable point-fix, the same as Members' three were this session.

### 2026-09-15 — Phase 2 pilot: Member/name column made non-sortable (backend validation gap found live, not assumed from the use case)

**Decided by:** Claude Code (Phase 2 pilot session)
**Context:** `GetAccountMembersUseCase`'s sort `switch` statement has cases for `"name"` and `"email"` in addition to `"roleLevel"`/`"joinedAt"`, suggesting all four are valid `sortBy` values. The initial column design (per the brief's click-to-sort instruction) made the "Member" column sortable with column id `'name'`, intending to send `sortBy=name`.
**Options considered:** (1) trust the use case's switch statement and ship `sortBy=name` as a valid, sortable column; (2) verify against the real running backend before shipping.
**Decision:** Option 2 — and it caught a real bug: the route's actual Zod validator, `getMembersQuerySchema` (`apps/api/src/modules/identity/application/dto/account.dto.ts`), only accepts `sortBy: z.enum(["roleLevel", "joinedAt"])`. A live `sortBy=name` request against the running local backend returns `400 VALIDATION_ERROR`, not a sorted result — the use case's `"name"`/`"email"` switch cases are dead, unreachable code. Fixed by making only Role (`roleLevel`) and Joined (`joinedAt`) sortable; Member (name/email/avatar) and Verified are not, since the backend has no matching sort field for either.
**Rationale:** This is exactly the kind of gap functional testing against the real backend is for — a plausible-looking use case implementation that the actual validated contract doesn't allow. Both working sort options (`roleLevel`, `joinedAt`) were verified live (ascending/descending, correct row order) before being shipped as sortable.
**Status:** active. If `GetAccountMembersUseCase`'s `"name"`/`"email"` sort branches are ever wanted, the fix belongs in `getMembersQuerySchema`'s enum (backend), not the frontend column definition — out of this session's scope.

### 2026-09-15 — apps/web gets its own vitest+jsdom+RTL test setup; run only via `pnpm --filter @vhyxvoid/web test`

**Decided by:** Claude Code (Phase 2 pilot session)
**Context:** The task brief's step 8 asked for tests covering the new props-based contract and click-to-sort behavior. `apps/web` had zero tests of any kind before this session — no vitest config, no `@testing-library/react`, nothing — and the root `tests/vitest.config.ts` runs backend-only tests in a Node environment (no jsdom, `include: tests/**/*.test.ts`), so it couldn't be extended to cover this.
**Options considered:** (1) skip automated component tests, rely on the manual functional check alone; (2) stand up a full jsdom+RTL setup scoped to `apps/web`, mirroring its existing "kept independent of root tooling" precedent (own tsconfig, own ESLint — 2026-09-09 entries); (3) add apps/web's test files to the root config instead.
**Decision:** Option 2. New `apps/web/vitest.config.ts` (jsdom, `@vitejs/plugin-react`, `vite-tsconfig-paths`) + `apps/web/vitest.setup.ts` + a `"test": "vitest run"` script. Option 3 was rejected outright — the root config's Node environment can't render React, and merging configs risks exactly the alias-resolution collision this session actually hit (see below).
**Two real cross-repo/tooling problems surfaced and worked around, both confirmed live, not guessed:**
1. **`postcss.config.mjs`'s `plugins: ['@tailwindcss/postcss']` shorthand** (a Next.js/webpack convention) breaks Vite's own `postcss-load-config`, which expects real plugin instances — every CSS Modules import (needed just for their class-name export map, regardless of `test.css`) fails to transform. Worked around with an inline `css.postcss: {plugins: []}` in `vitest.config.ts`, bypassing the project's postcss config entirely for tests (tests don't assert on actual Tailwind-generated styles).
2. **`@vhyxui/react` components can't mount under this repo's React at all** — deeper than the already-documented cross-repo dependency-duplication class of issue (2026-09-10 "VhyxUI Form/react-hook-form" entry): `@vhyxseal/react`'s `withAgentContract` HOC (wrapping Card/Badge/Button, likely every exported component — an addition to VhyxUI since that entry was written) pulls VhyxUI's own separately-installed React copy regardless of Vite's `resolve.dedupe`/`resolve.alias` (tried both; neither fixed it — the HOC's `require('react')` resolves from its own physical location in the sibling repo's `node_modules`, outside this project's dependency graph that `dedupe`/`alias` reason about). **Worked around, not fixed** (not fixable from this repo's side): `vi.mock('@vhyxui/react', ...)` per test file, stubbing the handful of components actually used with plain DOM elements. `resolve.dedupe`/`alias` were left in `vitest.config.ts` anyway since they're harmless and may help simpler, non-`@vhyxseal`-wrapped cases later.
3. **Vitest 4's workspace auto-discovery breaks both configs when invoked ambiguously**: a bare `npx vitest run` from inside `apps/web` (after `cd`) picked up the root `tests/vitest.config.ts` too (since both exist under the pnpm workspace), merged them into one run, and then failed to resolve `apps/web`'s own `@/` alias — the merged run doesn't reliably apply each project's own tsconfig-paths resolution. `pnpm --filter @vhyxvoid/web test` (which runs the package's own `"test"` script, `vitest run` with no args, from its own cwd via pnpm) does not trigger this and is confirmed clean. **This is now the only supported way to run apps/web's frontend tests** — documented in `vitest.config.ts`'s own comment so a future session doesn't rediscover it.
**Rationale:** All three are genuine, confirmed-live tooling gaps (not assumed), each with a minimal, narrowly-scoped workaround rather than a deeper fix that would be out of this session's scope (fixing VhyxUI's own `@vhyxseal` packaging, or Vitest's workspace-detection behavior, are not this repo's to fix).
**Status:** active. `pnpm --filter @vhyxvoid/web test` runs 3 files / 11 tests clean. Turbo's generic `test` pipeline task picks this up automatically (no `turbo.json` change needed) — confirmed via `turbo run test --filter=@vhyxvoid/web`. **Note for whoever next touches root-level tooling**: `apps/api`, `apps/hub`, and `apps/demo-backend` all have pre-existing, broken `test` npm scripts (`apps/api`: `vitest run` finds zero matching files in its own dir; `apps/hub`/`apps/demo-backend`: placeholder `echo "Error: no test specified" && exit 1`) — discovered incidentally while confirming `turbo run test --filter=@vhyxvoid/web` works in isolation, not fixed (out of this session's scope), but a real gap in `turbo run test` at the repo root today.

### 2026-09-15 — .claude/backlog.md created for small, already-diagnosed, non-urgent items

**Decided by:** Tanveer (directed), Claude Code (implementation)
**Context:** Several sessions' decision.md entries had accumulated small "flagged, not fixed" follow-ups (broken test scripts, a dead switch branch, cross-repo follow-up sessions still needed) that weren't big enough for context.md's numbered Known Risks/Gaps section but also weren't being tracked anywhere durable — they only lived in each session's own session_update.md report, easy to lose track of.
**Decision:** New `.claude/backlog.md` — a plain checklist, one line per item with a short context note and a source citation (session date or decision.md entry name). Seeded from a scan of decision.md and recent session_update.md entries, cross-checked against context.md's numbered items to avoid duplication (anything already numbered there stays there, not backlog.md). `.claude/claude.md`'s bootstrap instructions updated: read backlog.md alongside context.md/decision.md at the start of every session, and append new small deferred items there instead of letting them only live in a session's own report. Rule: when an item is fixed, delete its line entirely — never check it off and leave it, so the file only ever shows what's still outstanding.
**Rationale:** context.md's numbered list is for the big architectural questions this project's sessions keep re-deciding not to fix yet (auth-path divergence, no horizontal Hub scaling, etc.) — mixing small, mechanical follow-ups into that list would dilute it. A separate, deliberately lightweight file (no elaborate write-ups — if something needs a real explanation, it belongs in decision.md/context.md and backlog.md just points at it) keeps the small stuff visible without competing for attention with the big stuff.
**Status:** active. Seeded with 8 items (see backlog.md); one (the API Keys line) already partially closed out in the same session that created it, since Part 2 of this session's own brief converted API Keys.
